You reported a power analysis that has been classified as ‘post-hoc’. Calculating observed power is almost never useful. If you actually performed a sensitivity power analysis, label it as such explicitly.

power_id text
1 Ideally, we use the fastest evidence accumulating E-process, as it will provide us with the earliest option to stop data collection and potentially maximise resource conservation. We use this optimisation principle to select E-processes in Section 2.1 below. Section 2.3 focuses on how n plan in Protocol 1.1 can be chosen based on a power analysis. As with a classical power analysis, we assume to be given a desired power requirement, e.g. power = 80%, and an expected, or minimum clinically relevant, effect size δ min . Such a δ min is intuitively meaningful when we are not interested in tiny effects of (relatively) costly new treatments; for the case that we are, see Section 3.5. In two-sided settings we refer to data-governing effect sizes δ with |δ| ≥ δ min > 0 as clinically relevant.
2 For this reason we developed routines in the safestats package that efficiently sample the first-passage time distribution. Specifically, using (essentially) just Line 3 of R Code 2.1, we simulate (by default) m = 1000 e-value sequences under P δ min, all in under 2.4 seconds on a 2021 iMac M1. 14 Line 4 plots the first-passage time distribution as in 1 library ( safestats ) 2 3 designObj ← designSaviT ( deltaMin =0.5, beta =0.2, testType = ” twoSample ” ) 4 plot ( designObj ) The top panel of Fig. 7 illustrates the distinct roles of α, δ min, and the desired power for this power analysis. Firstly, α defines the minimum level of evidence for persuasive support, e.g. 1/α = 20, which is shown as the horizontal black line. Secondly, δ min defines the optimal mom E-process which is expressed by the steepness of the average upward drift of the e-value sequences. Lastly, the blue histogram shows that after n1 = n2 = 100 observations, a wee few more than 800 out of the m = 1000 e-value sequences correctly resulted in a first option to stop in favour of the presence of a treatment effect. Hence, by monitoring e n ≥ 1/α up to n plan = 100 we are able to detect a true treatment effect size of δ min with at least 80% power. To acknowledge that n plan is derived from simulations, the design object (Fig. 8) also reports twice the bootstrap standard error of 5.69. It also shows an average sample size of n mean = 59 for both groups, which is the average between the first times N < n plan at which e N ≥ 1/α occurs, and N = n plan for those e-value sequences (green) that continued until n plan . In comparison, a classical p < 0.05 test with the same α, power, δ min always requires n1 = n2 = 64. Hence, under P δ min, the mom savi t-test will require on average 5 fewer (but in the worst-case, 36 more) participants in both groups compared to the classical p < 0.05 test. The flexibility of e-value based tests comes at the price of a larger sample size to plan for, but Fig. 7 shows that in return there is about 59% probability to realise a stopped experiment before n = 64, whenever δ = δ min.
3 A reasonable estimate of the underlying standardised effect size based on the original findings (Gray & Wegner, 2009, Study 1a) is within (0.769, 0.872). 19 We err on the side of caution by using the lower bound for our power analysis. Furthermore, we can argue for a one-sided test (Grünwald & Koolen, 2025), as the working hypothesis deals with the offending adult man being perceived as more, not less, responsible compared to the offending baby. A classical power analysis shows that for δ min = 0.769 and power = 80%, the one-sided p-value test should be performed after gathering data from n1 = n2 = 22 participants in each group.
4;5;6 There were 57 Many Labs 2 replication attempts that fulfilled the inclusion criteria, of which 3 (5.26%) yielded a significant p-value less than α = 0.05. These conclusions were based on n1 = 3439 and n2 = 3508 participants. 22 A reasonable estimate of the datagoverning standardised effect size based on the original findings in Zhong and Liljenquist (2006, Study 2) is within (0.909, 1.429). As before, we err on the side of caution by using the lower bound for our design. A classical power analysis shows that for δ min = 0.909 and power = 80% power, the one-sided p < α test should be conducted after gathering data from n1 = n2 = 16 participants in each group. Lines 2 to 3 of R Code 4.1 with deltaMin <-(4.95-3.75)/1.32 show that with the correspondingly tuned mom E-process, we should plan for n plan = n1 = n2 = 27 participants in each group to observe e n ≥ 20 with 80% power under δ min = 0.909. If δ = δ min the procedure is expected to stop after testing n mean = 17 participants in each group. 23 For most replication attempts the one-sided e-value test hit 22 Unfortunately, we were unable to retrieve the correct “bogota” data from the data sets uploaded at https://osf.io/8cd4r/, which is why our results differ slightly compared to what is reported in Klein et al. (2018). 23 For a two-sided e-value test we require n plan = 33 and the procedure stops after nmean = 21 in each group n plan . We acquired e n ≥ 1/α = 20 in one out of a total of 57 replication attempts (1.75%) based on a total of n1 = 1427 and n2 = 1441 participants per group, thus, n1 = 25.04 and n2 = 25.28 on average. If the magnitude of the data governing δ was indeed at least δ min, then we have provided such δ ample chance to yield persuasive evidence. Since this did not occur for most replication attempts, we infer that the postulate δ ≥ δ min is unlikely with high probability. Moreover, we come to this conclusion with 2012 and 2067 fewer participants per group, which is a reduction of 58.5% and 58.9% from the total sample sizes used in the original analysis. We would like to reiterate the point that with e-values we do not have to stop at the first-passage time e n ≥ 1/α, nor at n plan . Due to E-processes being robust to any stopping time, there is no need to discard newly available data once the test is conducted or the confidence interval is computed, as is the case with a classical analysis.

9 There are circumstances where no minimal clinical relevant effect size δ min can be specified. Nonetheless, it is still possible to choose a tuning parameter and conduct a savi ttest. For instance, in a practical scenarios where the available budget forms the bottleneck of our investigation, yielding a restriction on the sample sizes, say, at most n1 = n2 = 40. Before running Protocol 1.1 with n plan = 40, we might want to get an indication of the δ that we can detect with, say, 80% power. Providing the designSaviT function with α, n plan, power, e.g. Lines 3 and 4 of R Code 2.1, reveals that the smallest effect that we can detect with 80% probability is about δ min = 0.882.
10 Subject experts might claim that an effect size of δ = 0.7 is more realistic. Such an effect can still be detected if we sample up to n1 = n2 = 40, but with smaller probability. Providing designSaviT with α, n plan, δ min, e.g. Lines 5 and 6 of R Code 2.1, we can conclude that under P δ with δ = 0.7 we have 64.2 % power to observe e n ≥ 20 by monitoring up to n plan = 40.
11 The fact that the grow E-process yields the lowest n plan is due to it being the fastest evidence accumulating procedure amongst all E-processes. However, in case the grow t-test was optimised for δ min = 0.5, but the data-governing effect size is actually δ = 0.2, then the grow E-process requires many more samples to yield e n ≥ 1/α with 80% power. If the grow E-process were tuned to δmin = 0.2 from the beginning, then it only needs n plan = 531 instead of n plan = 2612, which equates to a relative increase of 392%. The relative increase is much less for mom (36.6%), eGauss (9.2%), and eCauchy (8.0%), though, the latter two types have higher baselines (643 and 686 compared to 596 respectively), see also Fig. 9.
12 Upon reaching n plan without the e-value sequence crossing 1/α, we can halt data collection, maintain the null hypothesis as the status quo, and assert that the effect is not clinically relevant, with a type II error rate of at most 1 -power. Alternatively, we can also continue sampling if the e-value looks promising and we are keen to investigate a smaller effect. Similar conclusions can be reached by exploring the anytime-valid confidence intervals. The role of the planned sample size is to guarantee at least, e.g. power = 80% probability to gain persuasive support for a treatment effect under continuous monitoring. R Code 2.2: The design function designSaviT() takes as input α and any two of the three quantities power, δ min, n plan to yield an indication of the remaining quantity, and the optimal parameter as output. Lines 1 and 2 specify as input power, δ min and outputs n plan, Lines 3 and 4 take as input power, n plan and outputs an indication of δ min, Lines 5 and 6 take as input δ min, n plan and yield power, thus, expected power. Lines 7 and 8 take as input n plan and yield the parameter such that at n plan the confidence interval is the narrowest.
13 We recommend the mom E-process (Johnson & Rossell, 2010) as a default procedure, because the resulting savi t-test provides the best balance between efficiency and robustness to the choice of δ min, as a result of specifying a continuous prior as opposed to point priors (e.g. grow in purple in Fig. 4). It also yields well-concentrated anytime-valid confidence intervals (blue curves in Fig. 10). Table 2 shows n plan for various types of E-processes when α = 0.05 and power = 80% in the two-sample t-test setting with n = n1 = n 2 for δ min = 0.5 and δ min = 0.2. The ordering of the E-process types in terms of the lowest n plan remains the same for various values of α, power, δ min, e.g. Fig. 9. For a fair comparison between the four types of E-processes, we chose the fastest evidence accumulating E-process for each type (e.g. Section 1.3.5). The third and last columns also show the average sample sizes. For instance, under δ = δ min = 0.5, the first two rows show that the procedure, which monitors the grow (or mom) e-value up to n plan and stops as soon as e n ≥ 1/α, will, on average, stop after n mean = n1 = n2 = 56 (or n1 = n2 = 59) participants. For the same α, power and δ min, the classical p < α test should always be performed at n1 = n2 = 64 -even in the lucky situation when δ ≥ δ min . By contrast, all E-processes adapt to the advantageous situation, allowing experiments to be stopped even earlier, which further increases resource savings.
14 The mom savi t-test provides the best balance between efficiency and robustness to the choice of δ min . Except for the third and the last column with n mean above them, the table shows the planned sample sizes n1 = n 2 based on α = 0.05 and power = 80%. To acknowledge that these planned (and average) sample sizes are found by simulation we also included two times the bootstrap standard errors. The grow E-process has the lowest n plan for δ ≥ δ min, but requires an enormous number of samples to yield high evidence with, e.g. power = 80% probability under |δ| < δ min . The mom E-process is more robust to the choice of δ min and yields competitive n plan close to those of the grow E-process when |δ| ≥ δ min . The average sample sizes of grow and mom are lower than the sample sizes needed for a classical fixed sample size p-value test.
15 grow large when the null is false. As more data are collected under the alternative, the evalues should become increasingly pronounced, allowing the procedure to potentially achieve 100% statistical power (Ram & Ramdas, 2026). Fig. 5 illustrates this divergent behaviour by applying Protocol 1.1 with the mom type savi t-test, where g mom = 0.5 2 2 as introduced earlier, and the arbitrarily chosen time horizon n plan = 200. The left panel shows that sequences of e-values under the null have a tendency to decrease towards zero, whereas the right panel shows sequences where the data were generated under the alternative with standardised treatment effect δ = µ 1 -µ 2 -φ 0 σ set to δ = 0.5. Under the alternative, most of the e-value sequences (blue curves) increase rapidly. By quickly accumulating evidence against the null, we gain persuasive support (i.e. e n ≥ 1/α = 20) for a treatment effect sooner. In the full simulation 4921 out of 5000 e-value sequences pass 1/α = 20 by n plan = 200. Stopping as soon as e n ≥ 1/α = 20 leads to an average stopping time of n mean = 67.19. Thus, by time n plan = 200, this method correctly indicated persuasive evidence in favour of an effect in 98.42% of cases, demonstrating its high statistical power. An E-process with 100% power implies that the remaining e-value sequences will also cross 1/α, if we gather more data under the alternative. The opposing behaviour depicted by the two panels shows that a sequentially observed e n ≥ 1/α can genuinely be viewed as more likely under the alternative than under the null.

Power analyses need to contain the following information to be interpretable: the type of power analysis, the statistical test, the software used, sample size, critical alpha criterion, power level, effect size, and an effect size metric. In addition, it is recommended to make sure the power analysis is reproducible (by sharing the code, or a screenshot, of the power analysis), and to provide good arguments for why the study was designed to detect an effect of this size.

For an a-priori power analysis, where the sample size is determined, reporting all information would look like:

An a priori power analysis for an independent samples t-test, conducted using the pwr.t.test function from pwr (Champely, 2020), indicated that for a Cohen’s d = 0.5, an alpha level of 0.05, and a desired power level of 80% required at least 64 participants in each group.

For a sensitivity power analysis, this sentence would look like:

A sensitivity power analysis for an independent samples t-test, conducted using the pwr.t.test function from pwr (Champely, 2020), indicated that with 64 participants in each group, and an alpha level of 0.05, a desired power level of 80% was reached for an effect size of d = 0.5.

To fix Exact P-Values

What we found

We found 6 imprecise p values out of 10 detected p values.

Show details

Reporting p values imprecisely (e.g., p < .05) reduces transparency, reproducibility, and re-use (e.g., in p value meta-analyses). Best practice is to report exact p-values with three decimal places (e.g., p = .032) unless p values are smaller than 0.001, in which case you can use p < .001.

This module sometimes flags false positives when sentences discuss how p-value thresholds were set. It can also miss an imprecise p-value that is reported against a threshold (e.g., “decreased at the p < .05 significance threshold”).

P-Value Text
p < 0.05 procedures that monitors the p < 0.05 test and 95% confidence interval under repeated use.
p < 0.05 In comparison, a classical p < 0.05 test with the same α, power, δ min always requires n1 = n2 = 64.
p < 0.05 Hence, under P δ min, the mom savi t-test will require on average 5 fewer (but in the worst-case, 36 more) participants in both groups compared to the classical p < 0.05 test.
p < 0.05 This gain in efficiency goes unnoticed for the p < 0.05 test that for type I error control should always be performed at n = 64.
p < 0.05 Indeed, this procedure results in 48 null rejections at p < 0.05 out of a total of 61 replication attempts (78.7%).
p < 0.05 As an example stopping time we take the first time N at which p < 0.05, which corresponds to observing |x n | > 1.96 √ n .

The APA manual states: Report exact p values (e.g., p = .031) to two or three decimal places. However, report p values less than .001 as p < .001. However, 2 decimals is too imprecise for many use-cases (e.g., a p value meta-analysis), so report p values with three digits.

American Psychological Association (2020). Publication manual of the American Psychological Association, 7 edition. American Psychological Association.

README Files

README files are a way to document the contents and structure of a folder, helping users locate the information they need. You can use a README to document changes to a repository, and explain how files are named. Please consider adding a README to each repository or including ‘README’ in the name of your overview document.

Archive Files

The following files are not ZIP archives: safestats_0.8.8.tar.gz, safestats_0.8.8.tar.gz. We recommend the .zip format for archives: only ZIP stores its file listing in a way that lets a tool inspect the contents without downloading the whole archive, so a .zip is more discoverable and re-usable than a .7z, .rar, .tar or .tar.gz.

Study/Repository Mismatch

The studies named in the manuscript do not match how the repository’s files are grouped: the manuscript names studies (ex1a, ex2, ex69) with no matching files in the repository; the repository separates out a study (ex1) not named in the manuscript. Check that every study has its own clearly-named folder or file prefix, and that the folder names match how the manuscript refers to each study.

Code Comments

Best programming practice is to add comments to code, to explain what the code does (to yourself in the future, or peers who want to re-use your code). All your code files had comments.

Missing Files

All files loaded in the code were present in the repository.

Absolute Paths

Best programming practice is to use relative file paths (e.g., ‘./files’) instead of absolute file paths (e.g., ‘C://Lakens/project_dir/files’) as these folder names do not exist on other computers. No absolute file paths were found in any of the code files.

Working Directory (setwd)

Best programming practice is to avoid setwd() in analysis code: it hardcodes an assumption about the working directory (often an absolute path on the author’s own machine), so the code breaks when run anywhere else. Keep the working directory as the caller sets it and use relative paths. No setwd() calls were found in any of the code files.

Package Installation (install.packages)

Best programming practice is to avoid install.packages() in analysis code: it installs software on whoever runs the script without asking, which is at minimum disruptive (an unexpected install, network access, a package version the author never tested against) and at worst a security risk if it points at an untrusted repository. Document required packages instead (a README, or a list of them: an renv.lock/DESCRIPTION for R, a requirements.txt for Python). install.packages() calls were found in 3 code files.

Libraries / Imports

Best programming practice is to load all required libraries/imports in one block near the top of the code. In 1 code file, libraries/imports were at multiple places (i.e., with more than 3 non-comment lines in between).

Reproducible Environment

No renv.lock file, sessionInfo()/session_info() record, or groundhog/checkpoint date-pin call was found anywhere in the repository. Without one of these, the exact R and package versions used for the analysis are not recoverable, and package versions may drift between when this analysis was run and any later reproduction attempt. Consider depositing an renv.lock (via the renv package), a sessionInfo() text dump, or a groundhog/checkpoint date-pin alongside the code.

This module runs automated data-quality checks (empty columns, SPSS filter variables, category casing, stray whitespace, numbers stored as text, problematic and colliding column names, mixed text encodings, personal information, spreadsheet formatting, and careless responding) on the extracted data files.

We examined 54 columns across 4 data files.

Issues Identified

8 file/column combinations have at least one issue.

About the personal-information flags. These are pattern matches, not confirmed disclosures, and they are tuned to over-report rather than miss something. Expect false positives:

  • Column names are matched against identifying words in English and other European languages (email, telefoonnummer, geboortedatum, …). A column called phone_number flags whether or not it holds one, and an empty or already-anonymised column flags on its name alone.
  • Values are matched against the shape of an identifier. An IP address pattern also matches a version string or a dotted numeric code; a card-number pattern requires both a real issuer prefix and a checksum, but a long numeric ID can still coincide.
  • Free text is flagged when a column holds long, mostly-distinct prose — the shape open-ended answers take. Most such columns contain no names at all.

A flag means look at this column. Where the data is already de-identified (an ip column reading Anonymized, a name column the authors emptied), no action is needed.

Careless Responding

No file could be screened for careless responding.

What this does not cover. Only 0 of 4 data files could be screened: the rest had no detectable multi-item scale block, or too few respondents. Even in a screened file, these checks only look at multi-item rating scales — the only place where a repeated or flat answer pattern is interpretable. Single questions, open text, demographics and any item outside a detected scale block are never examined. A respondent can therefore answer most of a survey carelessly and still not be flagged here, and finding nothing is not evidence that a dataset is free of careless responding.

How to make your code run on another computer

We ran your code on a clean computer, the way a reader or reviewer would. Below is each problem we found, the scripts it affects, and how to fix it. Fix them in the order given: a problem near the top can cause the ones below it.

A package could not be installed.

  • 0.InstalGuide.R needs safestats.
  • a.figure1,H2,E1,H1,4,5.r needs safestats.
  • a.figure2,4,5,8,A1.R needs safestats.

safestats could not be installed in our run. Record the versions you used (in R, renv::init() writes them to a renv.lock file) and your R version, and say in the README where any package that is not on CRAN comes from.

A script uses something it does not create or load itself.

  • 0.figure1.R: designSaviT
  • b.figure3.R: setSafeStatsPlotOptionsAndReturnOldOnes
  • v2/b.figure3.R: setSafeStatsPlotOptionsAndReturnOldOnes
  • c.figure6.R: designSaviT
  • c.figure7.R: designSaviT
  • d.figure9.R: setSafeStatsPlotOptionsAndReturnOldOnes
  • e.figure9.R: designSaviZ
  • e.figure10.R: designSaviZ
  • f.figure10a.R: designSaviT
  • g.figure10b.R: designSaviT

Usually the object was still in your R session from earlier work, or another script made it.

  1. Restart R (in RStudio: Session > Restart R).
  2. Run each script from top to bottom, and fix what is missing.
  3. If a script needs results of another, save them to a file in the first script and read them in the second.
  4. Load every package with library() at the top.

File paths

Every data path in the code works in the repository as deposited. The code reads files that are only inside safestats_0.8.8.tar.gz, v2/safestats_0.8.8.tar.gz, and no script unpacks them. We unpacked them in their folder before the run. So that others can run the code as deposited, start the code with a line that unpacks them (such as unzip("safestats_0.8.8.tar.gz") in R), or upload the files unpacked.
