( 4) imply the following: Under any distribution from the null, there is at most α probability that an E-process produces misleading evidence exceeding 1/α upon stopping, no matter which (possibly unknown) stopping time is used to terminate data collection: For all data generating P from M 0 and any stopping time

Eq. ( 6) forms the basis for safe anytime-valid (henceforth savi) tests and confidence intervals. Since Eq. ( 6) holds for all stopping times, it does so for the ones mentioned in Section 1.3.1. Applying Eq. ( 6) to the first time N at which E n passes 1/α leads to Ville's inequality (see Howard, Ramdas, McAuliffe, and Sekhon (2020), Koolen, Pérez-Ortiz, and Lardy (2025), Ruf, Larsson, Koolen, and Ramdas (2023) and Appendix C for details):

For all P from M 0 ∶ P(There exist a time n such that

In contrast to Eq. ( 2) for p-values, Ville's inequality ensures that additional data can not nudge an E-process into favouring ineffective treatments with a probability above α. This robustness persists even if the extra data are gathered at the insistence of a hopeful reviewer or investigator, and even if data collection continues beyond the moment e n ≥ 1/α, since Eq. ( 6) still holds. For these reasons, we refer to the e n ≥ 1/α test as a savi test of level α.

Ville's inequality guarantees that the result of the procedure where we sequentially sample, peek, and stop as soon as the evidence crosses 1/α as described in Protocol 1.1 remains valid. The blue curve in the right panel of Fig. 2 applies this procedure, with

The sequential e-value testing procedure with finite time horizon n plan = 200. R Code 2.1 shows the simplified version of the code with an n plan based on a power analysis, and we refer to Section 2.3 for further details on the syntax. a so-called mom type savi t-test that we introduce in the next section, to the same data that led the p-value to incorrectly reject the null on 31 occasions. In contrast, the e-value sequence correctly remained under 1/α = 20 at all n = 200 time points.

The left panel of Fig. 3 illustrates in blue the (frequentist) guarantee of the procedure that tracks the mom type savi t-test that rejects the null as soon as e n ≥ 1/α = 20. Only 182 out of the 5000 e-value sequences (3.64%) contained a time point at which the e-value exceeded 1/α = 20. As the number of looks increases, so will the FPR, but only slightly. Ville's inequality guarantees that the FPR of the savi test will not exceed the tolerable 5%, even if the time horizon is increased indefinitely.

1.3.5 E-processes for t-tests

E-processes for composite M 0 (consisting of more than one distribution, as is the case for t-tests) are more complicated. The increase in complexity stems from requirement Eq. ( 5), which is harder to fulfil for a larger class of null-consistent probability distributions. There are, fortunately, various general approaches to construct E-processes for composite null models: (a) the universal inference construction that works for any M 0 that admits a (type of) maximum likelihood estimator (Wasserman, Ramdas, & Balakrishnan, 2020), (b) the reverse information projection approach (e.g. Grünwald, De Heide, & Koolen, 2024; Larsson, Ramdas, & Ruf, 2025) that is guaranteed to lead to powerful E-variables by optimisation, (c) the p-to-e-calibrator approach that uses specific functions to convert any p-value into an e-value (e.g. Shafer, Shen, Vereshchagin, & Vovk, 2011 , Vovk & Wang, 2021), (d) as well as other constructions based on, for instance, a betting interpretation (Waudby-Smith & Ramdas, 2024), and the Doob martingale (Koning & van Meer, 2025).

The first two constructions lead to expressions that can be immediate recognised as variations of likelihood ratios. 8 None of the generic constructions are as powerful as the approach by Pérez-Ortiz, Lardy, de Heide, and Grünwald (2024), who showed that for any savi test defining tuning parameter δ s the process where in the denominator f ν (t) is the likelihood of the (standard) T -distribution for outcome t with ν degrees of freedom, and where in the numerator

These variations are not the same as the classical concept of generalised likelihood ratios, since these do not form E-processes. Furthermore, they do not fully align with the notion of Bayes factors, as not all Bayes factors are E-variables, see Appendix I for an example, and not all E-variables are Bayes factors.

9 Eq. ( 8) makes intuitive sense in light of the result for simple M0. The major difference, however, is that the likelihood ratio for simple M0 are likelihood functions at the raw data x n . For the t-test M0 is composite, and the likelihood functions are at the reduced (and only marginally sufficient) T -statistic in both the numerator and denominator. This reduction leads to some challenges that make the rigorous proof of this result much more involved, see Pérez-Ortiz et al. (2024) and Lindon et al. (2026) and Appendix E for further details.

with the non-centrality parameter √ n eff δ s for outcome t realised from the usual T -statistic is the pooled sample deviation based on ν = n 1 + n 2 -2 degrees of freedom, and where 2 the effective sample size, and n k the sample size of group k = 1, 2.

Every choice of δ s in Eq. ( 8) leads to an E-process in the t-test setting. We are, however, not limited to choosing a single value, such as δ s = 0.5. Instead, we can spread our bet by taking a weighted average over the tuning parameter δ s and construct what is known as a mixture E-process with respect to a prior π(δ s ). For instance, using the point prior that puts all mass at δ s = 0.5 recovers the full commitment to the aforementioned single value. The prior is selected before data observation as in Bayesian statistics. However, we do not think of π as prior beliefs regarding δ s , but as a tuning distribution, which we optimise over, as elaborated on in Section 2 below; We further discuss how this approach contrasts with a Bayesian prior belief in the concluding Section 5.

Regardless of the chosen prior π, as long as the non-negative weights integrate or sum to one, that is, ∫ π(δ s )dδ s = 1, we get a mixture E-process comprised of E π n , where

Since Eq. ( 5) holds for each E δs n , it also holds for the mixture. To see this, let Ñ be an arbitrary stopping time, and P 0 any distribution from the null. Swapping the order of taking the expectation with respect to T , and the mixture/integral with respect to δ s yields

As this holds for any Ñ and P 0 from M 0 , we conclude that the mixture is indeed an Eprocess. Note that this argument does not depend on E δs N being a likelihood ratio. The general statement is that weighted averages of E-processes remain E-processes. This further hints at the candidate set of E-processes of M 0 being vast.

All illustrations and practical applications of anytime-valid inference discussed here are based on the safestats R package (Ly, Turner, et al., 2026). This package includes the following four types of mixture E-processes for the t-test setting when comparing • grow: This mixture uses two point priors for the tuning parameter in Eq. ( 8), assigning equal weight (e.g. 50%) to each of the values, say, δ s = -0.5 and δ s = 0.5.

The prior is visualised as the purple arrows in Fig. 4.

• eGauss: This set-up uses a zero-centred Gaussian prior N (0, g) on δ s (e.g. Gönen, Johnson, Lu, & Westfall, 2005). The dashed yellow curve in Fig. 4 illustrates the prior with g = 0.5 2.

-2 -1 0 1 2 0.0 0.2 0.4 0.6 0.8 Density Tuning parameter δ s Various priors for the construction of E-processes grow mom eCauchy eGauss

Figure 4

The various types of E-processes for the two-sided savi t-tests can be identified by the prior used to mix the T -likelihood ratio: mom is based on the non-local moment prior (blue), grow is based on the two point priors (purple arrows), eGauss is based on the Gaussian prior (dashed yellow), and eCauchy is based on the Cauchy prior (dashed brown) on δ s . All priors are optimised to a minimal clinically relevant effect size δ min = 0.5.

• mom: This mixture is based on a so-called non-local moment prior on δ s (e.g. Johnson & Rossell, 2010; Pramanik & Johnson, 2022). The corresponding two-bump/camel prior is plotted as the blue curve in Fig. 4, and the positions of the bumps take on the role of the tuning parameter controlled by g mom . Taking g mom = 0.5 2 2 mimics the grow choice at ±0.5.

• eCauchy: This mixes δ s with respect to a Cauchy prior δ s ∼ Cauchy(0, κ 2 ) (e.g. Jeffreys, 1961; Rouder, Speckman, Sun, Morey, & Iverson, 2009) with scale parameter κ represented by the dashed brown curve in Fig. 4, where κ = 0.5.

All four types of E-processes have in some form been introduced in the literature as Bayes factors (Jeffreys, 1961 , Ly, Verhagen, & Wagenmakers, 2016a , 2016b) with specifically chosen priors on the nuisance parameters, i.e. the global mean µ g = µ1 = µ 2 and σ (e.g. Gronau, Ly, & Wagenmakers, 2020), see also Appendix E. It is not well known that these Bayes factors are also E-variables for which Ville's inequality holds. This latter property suggests a frequentist-based application of these Bayes factors, which departs from their standard use; see Appendix J for a more detailed discussion.

1.3.6 Anytime-valid confidence intervals

Ville's inequality can be restated as follows: At least 1 -α proportion, e.g. 95%, of the e-value sequences will forever remain below 1/α, e.g. 20. This reformulation of Ville's inequality is derived by inverting Eq. ( 7), see Appendix F, and requires negating a "there exists" clause within the probability statement, which converts it into a "for all" clause, and vice versa, yielding:foot_4 For any possible data-generating P ∈ M 0 ∶ P(For all times n ∶ E n < 1/α) ≥ 1 -α.

(

This form of Ville's inequality is used to construct anytime-valid confidence intervals, that is, a sequence of confidence intervals that, with probability of a least 1 -α, will at every n cover the underlying true parameter of interest, here, the mean difference parameter φ = µ 1 -µ 2 . Prior to experimentation any value φ 0 on the real line is a candidate for the data-governing effect size. Each such φ 0 can serve as a null value in the T -statistic Eq. ( 9), which in turn affects the e-value at time n. By inverting E n < 1/α, we collect all those effect sizes φ 0 for which the associated e-values up to n have not (yet) exceeded 1/α. Since the inversion of E n < 1/α is carried out inside the probability statement of Eq. ( 12), the resulting intervals will at all times cover the true parameter φ with probability at least 1-α. These anytime-valid confidence intervals are therefore also attractive to those who suggest to eliminate the concept of statistical significance in favour of interval inference (Amrhein, Greenland, & McShane, 2019).

The bottom right panel of Fig. 2 shows an anytime-valid 95% confidence sequence. The horizontal yellow line depicts the true data generating φ = 0, which is within the intervals at all moments in time. While the sample mean difference estimator (centre of the intervals) may exhibit bias in the sequential setting, the intervals reliably include the true φ such as φ = 0, or φ = 1 in Fig. 10. The blue curve in the right-panel Fig. 3 shows that 4818 out of 5000 (96.36%) sequences of confidence intervals encapsulated the true data generating φ at all 200 looks. Ville's inequality Eq. ( 12) guarantees that the coverage probability remains above 95%, even if we extend the time horizon n plan indefinitely.

1.4 Cheating is still not allowed with e-values

Ville's and Markov's inequalities, Eq. ( 6), guarantee type I error control for any stopping time, but they offer no protection against data dredging practices. The crucial condition is that the decision to stop may depend only on the data observed so far -never on data still to come, but also not on data already in hand but selectively set aside.

Suppose a researcher collects n = 100 observations, shuffles them into a new order, notices that the e-value first crosses 1/α after m = 86 of the reshuffled observations, and reports e 86 while discarding the remaining 14. This is not a legitimate stopping time: the choice to stop at 86 was made with all 100 observations in hand, so it depends on "future" data. Dropping 14 observations as "outliers" because they lowered the e-value is the same violation in a different guise. 11 A second trap is reporting the running maximum. Imagine that the sequence e 1 , e 2 , . . . , e 100 peaked at n = 37 and never returned to that height. Ignoring X 38 , X 39 , . . . , X 100 and reporting the largest seen e 37 overstates the evidence against the null and carries some subtle consequences; see Appendix G for more details. Before collecting the observation at time n + 1, it is allowed to use the observations up to time n to decide which group to sample from next (Ly, Boehm, & Koolen, 2026). Validity breaks, however, if the group label is selected after the outcome at time n + 1 is known. Cherry-picking the E-process from the vast collection E(M 0 ) after seeing the data also undermines the claim that e n ≥ 1/α is rare under the null. Cheating is still prohibited within this framework of inference, and it is not anticipated to be permitted within any reasonable framework of inference (Steinhilber, Schnuerch, & Schubert, 2025).

The betting interpretation as described in Box 1 provides a heuristic to establish what remains valid with e-values. Before seeing the outcome at time n + 1, we are allowed to pick our betting strategy -thus, the mixture π and the savi test defining parametersbased on the information up to time n, but not after. Similarly, given data at round n, we can choose the roulette table to play on at time n + 1. After the outcome is revealed on our table, we cannot take our bet back and claim that we played on another table. Analogously, once we keep on playing and our wealth decreased, then we cannot walk off the table with the highest wealth that we gathered but lost along the way.

The remedy for all of these traps is the same: choose the E-process before data collection begins, and pre-register that choice. Below, we demonstrate how the safestats software package supports this practice.

Once an E-process is selected, Ville's and Markov's inequality Eq. ( 6) ensure that inference remains safe and anytime-valid. This is achieved by bounding the probability of an E-process producing misleading evidence, even under the most aggressive first-passage time rule, which rejects the null as soon as e n ≥ 1/α, while continually extending the time horizon to maximise the potential for misdirection.

Hence, instead of fixing the sample size in advance, we propose to use the datadriven procedure as described in Protocol 1.1 in R code. The first occurrence of e n ≥ 1/α does not mandate stopping or the rejection of the null hypothesis. Instead, it serves as the earliest signal to consider halting data collection as a result of persuasive evidence. Sampling can safely continue, allowing further data to potentially lower the e-value and revisit any preliminary hard conclusions regarding the null hypothesis. Similarly, we are free to continue sampling even if n plan is reached and more funding came available. Additional data always enhance inference, for example by tightening the mom type confidence intervals. In the end, the realised e-value is most naturally understood as a continuous measure of evidence against the null (e.g. Chugg et al., 2026; Koning, 2024).

2 Power and the fastest evidence accumulating E-process under alternatives

All mentioned savi t-tests (i.e. grow, mom, eGauss, and eCauchy) yield reliable inference with type I error guarantees, regardless of the chosen parameters. In this section, we show how to select the tuning parameter optimally under various practical constraints. The first part focuses on the prospective analysis to derive a non-binding indication of the budget we have to reserve for our experiments.The second part addresses the reverse problem of determining the power, or the minimal detectable effect size given a fixed budget, see Section 2.5.

For an E-process to quantify evidence effectively, it should not only generate evalues that remain forever small when the null holds true, but also produce sequences that Left panel: The mom type E-process starts at one and under the null yields sequences that slowly drift towards zero (yellow). From the depicted 100 runs, four e-value sequences (red/pink/orange/brown) led to misleading evidence. Right panel: Under the alternative δ = 0.5, the e-value sequences increase rapidly (blue). From the depicted 100 runs, two e-value sequences (dark green/ligth green) remained below 1/α = 20.

grow large when the null is false. As more data are collected under the alternative, the evalues should become increasingly pronounced, allowing the procedure to potentially achieve 100% statistical power (Ram & Ramdas, 2026). Fig. 5 illustrates this divergent behaviour by applying Protocol 1.1 with the mom type savi t-test, where g mom = 0.5 2 2 as introduced earlier, and the arbitrarily chosen time horizon n plan = 200. The left panel shows that sequences of e-values under the null have a tendency to decrease towards zero, whereas the right panel shows sequences where the data were generated under the alternative with standardised treatment effect δ = µ 1 -µ 2 -φ 0 σ set to δ = 0.5. Under the alternative, most of the e-value sequences (blue curves) increase rapidly. By quickly accumulating evidence against the null, we gain persuasive support (i.e. e n ≥ 1/α = 20) for a treatment effect sooner. In the full simulation 4921 out of 5000 e-value sequences pass 1/α = 20 by n plan = 200. Stopping as soon as e n ≥ 1/α = 20 leads to an average stopping time of n mean = 67.19. Thus, by time n plan = 200, this method correctly indicated persuasive evidence in favour of an effect in 98.42% of cases, demonstrating its high statistical power. An E-process with 100% power implies that the remaining e-value sequences will also cross 1/α, if we gather more data under the alternative. The opposing behaviour depicted by the two panels shows that a sequentially observed e n ≥ 1/α can genuinely be viewed as more likely under the alternative than under the null.

Ideally, we use the fastest evidence accumulating E-process, as it will provide us with the earliest option to stop data collection and potentially maximise resource conservation. We use this optimisation principle to select E-processes in Section 2.1 below. Section 2.3 focuses on how n plan in Protocol 1.1 can be chosen based on a power analysis. As with a classical power analysis, we assume to be given a desired power requirement, e.g. power = 80%, and an expected, or minimum clinically relevant, effect size δ min . Such a δ min is intuitively meaningful when we are not interested in tiny effects of (relatively) costly new treatments; for the case that we are, see Section 3.5. In two-sided settings we refer to data-governing effect sizes δ with |δ| ≥ δ min > 0 as clinically relevant.

2.1 Determining the fastest evidence accumulating E-variable under P δ min

For efficient inference, we prefer the procedure that has a higher tendency to yield large e-values on average. Suppose that E s 1 n and E s 2 n are two E-variables with savi test defining tuning parameters s 1 and s 2 , respectively.

Under an alternative P δ , we prefer E s 1 n over E s 2 n , if we expect E s 1 n to, say, triple, whereas E s 2 n to only double the evidence against the null at n. In general, we favour inference based on E s 1 n over E s 2 n , if the rate of growth of E s 1 n exceeds that of E s 2 n , which we capture using the logarithmic function. That is, if E

If the data are indeed generated under P δ , then the most preferable E n from E n (M 0 ), the collection of all E-variables at time n, is the one that achieves the following maximum (Grünwald, De Heide, & Koolen, 2024; Koolen & Grünwald, 2022

The E-variable that attains this maximum is referred to as the growth-rate optimal Evariable under P δ . Intuitively, an optimal choice maximises the rate at which the E-variable accumulates evidence, thereby minimising the average sample size required to achieve persuasive support under an effect of size δ (Ter Schure et al., 2024).

The optimal E-variable that solves Eq. ( 13), however, typically depends on the datagoverning δ. Due to not knowing P δ in practice, we cannot optimally specify the growth-rate optimal E-variable. One workaround involves a minimal clinically relevant effect size δ min and the adoption of a conservative approach. The so-called grow (the growth-rate optimal in worst case) E-variable then solves the following maximin problem max

As before, the maximum is over the vast collection E n (M 0 ), and the minimum (the worstcase part) is over all (alternative) data-generating distributions P δ that are clinically relevant. For models that have the so-called monotone likelihood property, the problem gets easier as the data-governing δ is further away from the null. For these models the inner minimum of Eq. ( 14) is attained by δ = δ min and simplifies to the problem Eq. ( 13). This is the case for t-tests. -Ortiz et al. (2024) showed that for t-tests, the one-sided grow E-variable (alternative δ ≥ δ min ) is given by δ s in Eq. ( 8) set equal to δ min . It follows that the two-sided grow E-variable is based on the two-point mixture that puts half its mass at δ s = -δ min and at δ s = δ min , see the purple arrows in Fig. 4. Importantly, this grow result is not a consequence of modelling subjective belief, but emerges from the desire to use the fastest evidence accumulating procedure. Left panel: Under the null, the sampling distribution of E-variables does not increase and typically drift towards zero. Right panel: Under the alternative, here with δ = δ min = 0.5, the distribution of mom e-values increases rapidly. Since mom is tuned to δ min and thus accumulates the evidence fastest, we know that the depicted increase is the steepest amongst the mom E-variables.

2.2 Fastest evidence accumulating E-processes in the t-test setting

Pérez

Instead of optimising over the full collection E(M 0 ), we can restrict the candidate set of E-variables to those belonging to the mom, eGauss, or eCauchy types E-variables in Eq. ( 14) resulting in optimal parameter values of g mom = δ 2 min 2 , g = δ 2 min , and κ = |δ min | respectively. 13 The right panel of Fig. 6 depicts in blue the sampling distribution of the mom savi t-test with g = δ 2 min 2 as a function of n for data generated under δ = δ min = 0.5. The 95% and 5% quantiles are depicted as dark blue curves, and the mean of the logarithm of the E-variables as the black curve, which all continue to increase as n grows. For completeness, the sampling distribution under the null is shown in yellow. This stark contrast in the behaviour of the E-variable under the alternative and null suggests that e-value sequences have the potential to remain self-consistent and non-contradictory over time under either hypotheses.

The selected mom E-process being optimal for the given δ min implies that the slope of the black curve in the right panel of Fig. 6 is the steepest achievable amongst all mom E-variables under P δ min with δ min = 0.5. As such, it is guaranteed to cross any evidence threshold 1/α (e.g. dashed horizontal grey line) under P δ min the soonest. Provided with a desirable power, e.g. 80% we can also determine when this crossing occurs.

2.3 Determining n plan for a desired power, δ min and tolerable α

The intuition behind the derivation of n plan in a power analysis is to horizontally cut the blue sampling distribution depicted in the right panel of Fig. 6 at 1/α. This is equivalent to studying the distribution of the first times at which the E-process passes 1/α, which we approximate by sampling.

For this reason we developed routines in the safestats package that efficiently sample the first-passage time distribution. Specifically, using (essentially) just Line 3 of R Code 2.1, we simulate (by default) m = 1000 e-value sequences under P δ min , all in under 2.4 seconds on a 2021 iMac M1. 14 Line 4 plots the first-passage time distribution as in 1 library ( safestats ) 2 3 designObj ← designSaviT ( deltaMin =0.5 , beta =0.2 , testType = " twoSample " ) 4 plot ( designObj ) The top panel of Fig. 7 illustrates the distinct roles of α, δ min , and the desired power for this power analysis. Firstly, α defines the minimum level of evidence for persuasive support, e.g. 1/α = 20, which is shown as the horizontal black line. Secondly, δ min defines the optimal mom E-process which is expressed by the steepness of the average upward drift of the e-value sequences. Lastly, the blue histogram shows that after n1 = n2 = 100 observations, a wee few more than 800 out of the m = 1000 e-value sequences correctly resulted in a first option to stop in favour of the presence of a treatment effect. Hence, by monitoring e n ≥ 1/α up to n plan = 100 we are able to detect a true treatment effect size of δ min with at least 80% power. To acknowledge that n plan is derived from simulations, the design object (Fig. 8) also reports twice the bootstrap standard error of 5.69. It also shows an average sample size of n mean = 59 for both groups, which is the average between the first times N < n plan at which e N ≥ 1/α occurs, and N = n plan for those e-value sequences (green) that continued until n plan . In comparison, a classical p < 0.05 test with the same α, power, δ min always requires n1 = n2 = 64. Hence, under P δ min , the mom savi t-test will require on average 5 fewer (but in the worst-case, 36 more) participants in both groups compared to the classical p < 0.05 test. The flexibility of e-value based tests comes at the price of a larger sample size to plan for, but Fig. 7 shows that in return there is about 59% probability to realise a stopped experiment before n = 64, whenever δ = δ min.

2.4 The behaviour of n plan under other alternatives

Monitoring the test is even more beneficial when the data-governing effect size is larger than δ min . The middle panel of Fig. 7 shows that, under P δ with δ = 0.6, the evidence against the null accumulates even faster, and the whole first-passage time distribution is shifted to the left. The average sample size at which persuasive support is gathered is then First-passage distribution of the mom E-process under0.45= δ < δ min =0.5

Figure 7

Regardless of the actual value of the data-governing δ, it can (eventually) be detected with a mom savi test. From top to bottom: Distribution of the first-passage distribution when the data-governing δ = δ min = 0.5, δ = 0.6 > δ min , and δ = 0.45 < δ min , respectively. Screenshot of the design object created on Line 3 in R Code 2.1.

at n mean = 46. This gain in efficiency goes unnoticed for the p < 0.05 test that for type I error control should always be performed at n = 64.

On the other hand, if the data-governing effect size is smaller than expected, then more samples are needed to observe e n ≥ 1/α with 80% power. The bottom panel of Fig. 7 shows that under P δ with δ = 0.45 about 69.5% of the e-value sequences led to e n ≥ 1/α at n = 100. Hence, although the n plan was tuned to the case δ = δ min , it also suffices for all data-governing δ larger than δ min , as one would intuit for a minimal clinically relevant effect size, see Section 3.5 for further discussion.

Upon reaching n plan without the e-value sequence crossing 1/α, we can halt data collection, maintain the null hypothesis as the status quo, and assert that the effect is not clinically relevant, with a type II error rate of at most 1 -power. Alternatively, we can also continue sampling if the e-value looks promising and we are keen to investigate a smaller effect. Similar conclusions can be reached by exploring the anytime-valid confidence intervals. The role of the planned sample size is to guarantee at least, e.g. power = 80% probability to gain persuasive support for a treatment effect under continuous monitoring. R Code 2.2: The design function designSaviT() takes as input α and any two of the three quantities power, δ min , n plan to yield an indication of the remaining quantity, and the optimal parameter as output. Lines 1 and 2 specify as input power, δ min and outputs n plan , Lines 3 and 4 take as input power, n plan and outputs an indication of δ min , Lines 5 and 6 take as input δ min , n plan and yield power, thus, expected power. Lines 7 and 8 take as input n plan and yield the parameter such that at n plan the confidence interval is the narrowest.

There are circumstances where no minimal clinical relevant effect size δ min can be specified. Nonetheless, it is still possible to choose a tuning parameter and conduct a savi ttest. For instance, in a practical scenarios where the available budget forms the bottleneck of our investigation, yielding a restriction on the sample sizes, say, at most n1 = n2 = 40. Before running Protocol 1.1 with n plan = 40, we might want to get an indication of the δ that we can detect with, say, 80% power. Providing the designSaviT function with α, n plan , power, e.g. Lines 3 and 4 of R Code 2.1, reveals that the smallest effect that we can detect with 80% probability is about δ min = 0.882.

Subject experts might claim that an effect size of δ = 0.7 is more realistic. Such an effect can still be detected if we sample up to n1 = n2 = 40, but with smaller probability. Providing designSaviT with α, n plan , δ min , e.g. Lines 5 and 6 of R Code 2.1, we can conclude that under P δ with δ = 0.7 we have 64.2 % power to observe e n ≥ 20 by monitoring up to n plan = 40.

If any of these prospective analyses show that (a) the planned sample size is too high, (b) the smallest detectable effect size is unrealistically large, or (c) the power is too low, then we can either request more funds to invite the derived additional number of participants to the study, or decide, in advance, that it is futile to conduct this experiment, and spend our time and efforts on different endeavours instead.

If neither δ min nor a desired power is known in advance, we can run designSaviT with only α, n plan , e.g. Lines 7 and 8 of R Code 2.1. The underlying code then finds the parameter value that at the specified n plan has the narrowest confidence interval. For n plan = n1 = n2 = 40 this yields g mom = 0.134. It is worth noting that the associated mom confidence interval becomes even narrower if we continue sampling. For instance, at n plan = 40 the narrowest mom confidence width is 1.315 attained by g mom = 0.134, if s2 = 1. But at n1 = n2 = 100 the interval width is shrunk to 0.85. Running Line 8 of R Code 2.1 with n plan = n1 = n2 = 100 reveals that the narrowest mom interval is attained by g mom = 0.05193, Lastly, if no information is given regarding δ min or an n plan , as on Line 9 in Protocol 1.1, then, by lack of better choice, a "default" mom type E-process with tuning parameter g mom = 0.5 2 2 is used for inference. This choice works well for a 'medium' effect size δ min = 0.5 according to Cohen (1988). Regardless of the chosen parameter, we can safely monitor the savi t-test as data accumulate and (relatively quickly) learn the underlying effect size without compromising the validity of the results.

The key point is that prospective power analyses for savi t-tests can be done with just a few lines of code. The resulting designObj defines the type of E-process to use, the tuning parameter for inference, and the duration over which the null hypothesis should be tested. To prevent concerns about cherry-picking, this information (such as which type of E-process is chosen for inference) can be saved and submitted to an online pre-registration repository before conducting the experiment. Importantly, the planned sample sizes are indicative only and do not represent a binding commitment regarding the number of samples to be collected. The realised sample size at which the experiment can be stopped can be smaller(larger) than planned, when the true effect is larger(smaller) than minimal clinically relevant. Regardless of the specific value of the data-governing δ, we can safely adapt our experiments to the available evidence, as quantified by the e-value.

3 Practical guidance for selecting amongst various types of E-processes

In Section 1.3.5 we listed four types of E-processes in the t-test setting. Each type provides type I error control over time, though some are better suited for certain purposes than others. In this section, we provide insights that guide the choice of the savi t-test. Table 1 summarises the advantages and disadvantages of the different priors.

3.1 Default choice: The mom E-process

We recommend the mom E-process (Johnson & Rossell, 2010) as a default procedure, because the resulting savi t-test provides the best balance between efficiency and robustness to the choice of δ min , as a result of specifying a continuous prior as opposed to point priors (e.g. grow in purple in Fig. 4). It also yields well-concentrated anytime-valid confidence intervals (blue curves in Fig. 10). Table 2 shows n plan for various types of E-processes when α = 0.05 and power = 80% in the two-sample t-test setting with n = n1 = n 2 for δ min = 0.5 and δ min = 0.2. The ordering of the E-process types in terms of the lowest n plan remains the same for various values of α, power, δ min , e.g. Fig. 9. For a fair comparison between the four types of E-processes, we chose the fastest evidence accumulating E-process for each type (e.g. Section 1.3.5). The third and last columns also show the average sample sizes. For instance, under δ = δ min = 0.5, the first two rows show that the procedure, which monitors the grow (or mom) e-value up to n plan and stops as soon as e n ≥ 1/α, will, on average, stop after n mean = n1 = n2 = 56 (or n1 = n2 = 59) participants. For the same α, power and δ min , the classical p < α test should always be performed at n1 = n2 = 64 -even in the lucky situation when δ ≥ δ min . By contrast, all E-processes adapt to the advantageous situation, allowing experiments to be stopped even earlier, which further increases resource savings.

Under δ = δmin = 0.2, we get average sample sizes of n mean = 323 and 344 participants in both groups for the grow and mom savi t-test, respectively, whereas the classical p < α test should always be performed at n1 = n2 = 394. Hence, both the grow and mom E-process will on average outperform the classical p-value test.

The fact that the grow E-process yields the lowest n plan is due to it being the fastest evidence accumulating procedure amongst all E-processes. However, in case the grow t-test was optimised for δ min = 0.5, but the data-governing effect size is actually δ = 0.2, then the grow E-process requires many more samples to yield e n ≥ 1/α with 80% power. If the grow E-process were tuned to δmin = 0.2 from the beginning, then it only needs n plan = 531 instead of n plan = 2612, which equates to a relative increase of 392%. The relative increase is much less for mom (36.6%), eGauss (9.2%), and eCauchy (8.0%), though, the latter two types have higher baselines (643 and 686 compared to 596 respectively), see also Fig. 9.

Table 2

The mom savi t-test provides the best balance between efficiency and robustness to the choice of δ min . Except for the third and the last column with n mean above them, the table shows the planned sample sizes n1 = n 2 based on α = 0.05 and power = 80%. To acknowledge that these planned (and average) sample sizes are found by simulation we also included two times the bootstrap standard errors. The grow E-process has the lowest n plan for δ ≥ δ min , but requires an enormous number of samples to yield high evidence with, e.g. power = 80% probability under |δ| < δ min . The mom E-process is more robust to the choice of δ min and yields competitive n plan close to those of the grow E-process when |δ| ≥ δ min . The average sample sizes of grow and mom are lower than the sample sizes needed for a classical fixed sample size p-value test.

To produce persuasive support for δ with 80% power, the savi tests require higher (worstcase) planned sample sizes, compared to the fixed sample p < α test under δ = δ min . On average the grow and mom savi t-tests outperform the classical version, and the gain is higher when the data-governing δ = δ min is smaller. Left panel: The dotted thin red line represents the number of samples needed for the classical p-value test to reject the null with 80% under δ shown on the x-axis. The four dotted lines at the top represent the worst-case n plan of eCauchy (brown), eGauss (yellow), mom (blue) and grow (purple). The solid lines represent the average sample sizes. Right panel: The same information as in the left panel is shown, but scaled so the p < α sample sizes are set to 100% representing the baseline. Roughly speaking, the additional data for the worst-case planning stage required for eCauchy (brown) is 82%, for eGauss (yellow) is 69%, for mom (blue) is 58%, and for grow (purple) is 42%. We require for eCauchy (brown) 12% and for eGauss (yellow) 6% more data on average, whereas for mom (blue) we require 4%, and for grow (purple) 10% less data.

3.2 Fast detection: The grow E-process

If the focus is on sequentially detecting an effect as quickly as possible, then there is a case to be made for the grow savi t-test. It has the theoretical desirable property of being the fastest evidence accumulating E-variable in the worst case amongst all Evariables for the M 0 of interest. Table 2 suggests the grow choice when δ smaller than δ min are truly uninteresting or impossible to measure due to the limits of our measurement instruments. Unfortunately, this grow E-process comes with the additional caveat that its associated anytime-valid confidence interval stops shrinking after a certain sample size. If it can be guaranteed that sampling will not exceed a certain sample size, then grow confidence intervals can still be reasonable. This, however, might be hard to guarantee in practice.

3.3 Eventually narrowest anytime-valid confidence interval: The eGauss Eprocess

One of the major advantage of the eGauss E-process is that both the two-sided e-value and the anytime-valid confidence interval have an explicit form, and are therefore a tad less hard to analyse (H. Wang & Ramdas, 2025). If the goal is to eventually get the most precise inference regarding the magnitude of the effect, then the eGauss savi t-test can be recommended. Listed in order of widest to narrowest at n = 1024, Fig. 10 shows the confidence intervals associated with grow in purple, mom in blue, eCauchy in red, and eGauss in yellow. The differences between mom, eCauchy and eGauss are hardly visible.

1 2 4 8 16 32 64 128 256 512 1024 -1 0 1 2 3 Mean difference Number of looks/Sample size Comparison of confidence sequences for different E-processes grow mom eCauchy eGauss

Figure 10

The confidence interval associated to the grow (purple) E-process stops shrinking after a certain sample size. The other confidence intervals shrink and are hard to distinguish from each other. Listed in order of widest to narrowest at n = 1024 we have: grow in purple, mom in blue, eCauchy in red, and eGauss in yellow. The data were generated with a true mean difference of φ = 1. This (eventually) narrower confidence width comes at a cost in terms of somewhat larger planned sample sizes for the test compared to those of grow and mom. Note that this choice for eGauss relies on long term gains, as it does not yield the narrowest interval at all times. For instance, the grow interval is the narrowest between n = 16 and n = 64.foot_10

3.4 Information consistent inference: The eCauchy E-processes

Of the listed E-processes, only the eCauchy savi t-test is information consistent. It will therefore yield irrefutable evidence against the null in case the data are overwhelmingly informative. For the case at hand, overwhelmingly informative data correspond to observing a non-zero sample mean difference without any sampling variability, see Gronau et al. (2020), and Ly et al. (2016a Ly et al. ( , 2016b) ) for more details. When it comes to n plan eCauchy performs relatively poorly, while its confidence interval width is close to that of eGauss. Another reason to choose eCauchy would be its overall robustness to the specification of δ min.

3.5 On δ min and early stopping

Table 2 shows that choosing δ min close to the data generating δ can result in fast detection of |δ| ≥ δ min , but a relatively harsh penalty in terms of n plan whenever |δ| < δ min . This is not a reason to choose δ min as small as possible, as a smaller δ min yields a larger n plan , which in turn leads to wasteful testing whenever the null holds true, see Fig. 9. The increase in n plan for |δ| < δ min should not pose a problem if δ min truly represents the minimal clinically relevant effect size. A meta-analysis of previous studies can help establish δ min , or it can be informed by an original finding that we aim to replicate, see Section 4.

Nevertheless, it may happen that no earlier findings are available, and the focus is on detecting even very tiny deviations from the null: every δ ≠ 0 would be 'relevant' and there really is no obvious choice for δ min (see (Ek et al., 2023; Lindon et al., 2026; Q. Wang et al., 2026) for some applications with this flavour). The situation is then arguably exploratory, whereas the discussion regarding priors and tuning parameters above is concerned with a prospective analysis. Regardless of the specific method (e.g. grow, mom, eGauss, or eCauchy) and the tuning parameters, all savi t-tests remain reliable, in the sense that they provide type I error guarantees. Regardless of the tuning parameters, the mom, eGauss, and eCauchy methods put non-zero prior mass on every region in the parameter space. As a consequence, in addition to providing type I error guarantees, they adapt to the value of the underlying effect size, no matter how small, as data accumulate. Therefore, if the actual δ ≠ 0 and we keep sampling data, they will eventually reject the null; in other words, for large sample sizes, their power tends to 1. For instance, we can safely use the mom E-process with tuning parameter g mom = 0.5 2 2 , which is used when no design object is specified. In particular, while 0.5 is the optimal choice if δ = 0.5, the resulting test will still reject after sufficiently many samples, for any other δ ≠ 0.5, as long as δ > 0. The sample size at which we reject under a very small but non-zero δ will tend to be somewhat smaller though for eGauss and eCauchy equipped with the corresponding tuning parameter, simply because they put much larger prior mass near δ = 0. On the other hand, if we use grow, then for such very small δ ≠ 0 it is possible that we never reject, no matter how large our sample size, simply because it puts prior mass 0 in a neighbourhood around δ = 0; grow will only tend to reject at with power going to one if the actual δ is larger than the δ min plugged into the tuning parameter. Superficially, it may seem that some frequentist methods can deal with arbitrarily small δ ≠ 0 without any additional tuning. But in fact they need tuning just as much as our E-processes. For example, the p < α test can be computed without the specification of a tuning parameter in the t-test setting -but only once we have decided on a sample size n. But to determine an n which will give us any reasonable power, we need an assumption on δ min after all; and worse, unlike for the mom, eGauss, and eCauchy E-processes, if our guess was too optimistic, we cannot continue sampling once we have exhausted our n initially planned points.

An alternative solution to taking a standard tuning parameter value such as g mom = 0.5 2 2 above, would be to run a pilot study, estimate the underlying effect size and use that as δ min in the designSaviT function. On the other hand, if we are constrained by other quantities such as our budget for n plan , then insights can be gathered with the methods described in Section 2.5. If we are interested in detecting arbitrarily small δ ≠ 0 in the prospective setting, and we have reached n plan without a rejection, we may accept the null for the time being, and hope that in the future we may obtain additional resources to allow us to continue collecting data. On the other hand, if we do have a well-motivated choice for δ min , we may sometimes stop early because we can simultaneously reject all δ with |δ| ≥ δ min ; this would happen if all these larger δs fall outside an anytime-valid confidence interval, for instance, this happens in Fig. 10 for δ min = 1.5. In this case,foot_11 we have established, with 1 -α confidence, that even if the null is false, the corresponding effect is uninteresting. We may then stop the analysis early, and sometimes save a considerable amount of resources, since further data collection becomes futile, see Section 5.2 for further discussion.

4 Two real-world examples illustrating e-value based inference in action

We show how inference can be sped up with e-values by examining two replication attempts from the Many Labs 2 Project (Klein et al., 2018), which investigated variations across samples and settings in the replicability of 28 classic psychological findings.

4.1 Example 1: Moral typecasting (Gray & Wegner, 2009, Study 1a)

Moral typecasting is the process where a moral agent (doer of right or wrong actions) is less likely to be perceived as a receiver of that action, and vice versa. Gray and Wegner (2009) postulated that age plays a key role in perceived morality. More precisely, they hypothesise that children are viewed as being less responsible for their actions. 18In the original experiment 69 participants read a story about either an adult man (high in moral agency) harming a baby, or a baby (low in moral agency) harming an adult man by knocking over a tray of glasses. Participants rated the responsibility of the offender on a 7-point scale from 1 (low) to 7 (high). On average, participants who read the story of the offending adult man rated him as more responsible (x1 = 5.29, s1 = 1.86) compared to participants who were presented with the offending baby (x2 = 3.86, s2 = 1.64). It was concluded that the observed mean difference was significant due to observing t(68) = 3.32 and p = 0.001 < α.

Out of 61 Many Labs 2 replication attempts, a total of 58 (95.08%) led to a significant p-value. The pre-registration required each replication attempt to contain at least 80 participants, say, n1 = n2 = 40 in each group. To ensure that the classical p-value tests remain trustworthy, data collection across all attempts were first stopped before the p-value tests were conducted. These conclusions were based on n1 = 4028 and n2 = 3974 participants in the two groups.

A reasonable estimate of the underlying standardised effect size based on the original findings (Gray & Wegner, 2009, Study 1a) is within (0.769, 0.872). 19 We err on the side of caution by using the lower bound for our power analysis. Furthermore, we can argue for a one-sided test (Grünwald & Koolen, 2025), as the working hypothesis deals with the offending adult man being perceived as more, not less, responsible compared to the offending baby. A classical power analysis shows that for δ min = 0.769 and power = 80%, the one-sided p-value test should be performed after gathering data from n1 = n2 = 22 participants in each group.

Lines 1 to 3 of R Code 4.1 show that with e-values we should plan for n plan = n1 = 1 deltaMin ← (5.29 -3.86) / 1.86 2 designObj ← designSaviT ( deltaMin = deltaMin , beta =0.2 , seed =1 , 3 testType = " twoSample " , alternative = " greater " ) 4 plot ( designObj ) n2 = 37 participants in each group to observe evidence against the null larger than 20 with 80% probability under δ min = 0.769. 20 The procedure that samples until this n plan or the first time e n ≥ 1/α will then on average stop after n mean = 22 participants in each group, if δ = δ min.

We compare the two designs side by side. For the classical analysis we pretend that for each replication attempt, we sample up to n1 = n2 = 22, stop the experiment, and then compute the one-sided p-value. 21 By reducing the sample sizes, we are going to have less power to reject the null. Indeed, this procedure results in 48 null rejections at p < 0.05 out of a total of 61 replication attempts (78.7%). For this conclusion, we used data from a total of n1 = 1315 of n2 = 1324 participants, thus, on average n1 = 21.56 and n2 = 21.71 in each replication attempt. In other words, this classical design required 2713 and 2650 fewer participants.

The one-sided e-value test allows for informed conclusions with even less data. Tracking the e-value up to n plan = 37 or stopping as soon as e n ≥ 20 yields 51 times persuasive evidence for the effect, out of a total of 61 replication attempts (83.6%). This evidence is gathered with a total of n1 = 1031 and n2 = 1062 participants, corresponding to an average of n1 = 16.90 and n2 = 17.41 per replication attempt. Compared to the original set-up, stopping as soon as e n ≥ 1/α = 20 reduced the number of participants by 2997 in one group and 2912 in the other, resulting in roughly 73% resource savings.

This demonstration highlights the benefits of planned analyses in general, and efficiency of e-value based tests in particular. The general conclusion remains the same if we tuned the E-process to the upper bound for δ min instead. Hence, the precise specification of δ min is not necessary to gain efficiency and conserve resources.

The left panel of Fig. 11 shows in blue the evolution of the e-value sequences that hit 1/α = 20, and in green the sequences that did not reach this threshold before n plan = 37. Left panel: 83.6% of the e-value sequences (blue) of the Gray and Wegner (2009) ManyLabs2 replication attempt reached 1/α = 20 before n plan = 37, whereas the green sequences did not. The green part of the stopping histogram corresponds to an e-value sequence that did not reach 1/α = 20. Right panel: 98.2% of the e-value sequences (green) of the Zhong and Liljenquist (2006) ManyLabs2 replication attempt did not reach 1/α = 20. There was one sequence that hit 1/α = 20.4.2 Example 2: The Macbeth effect -Moral violations and desire for cleansing (Zhong & Liljenquist, 2006, Study 2) and Liljenquist (2006) hypothesised that a threat to one's moral purity induces the need to (physically) cleanse oneself, which they referred to as the "Macbeth effect". In Study 2 a total of 27 participants copied a first-person account of an ethical act (helping a co-worker), or an unethical act (sabotaging a co-worker). Afterwards, the participants rated the desirability of five cleaning products and five non-cleaning products on a scale from 1 (not at all) to 7 (very much). Participants who copied the unethical story (x1 = 4.95, s1 = 0.84) found the cleaning products more desirable compared to participants who copied the ethical story (x2 = 3.75, s2 = 1.32). The null hypothesis of no effect was rejected based on t(25) = 2.64 and p = 0.01 < α = 0.05.

There were 57 Many Labs 2 replication attempts that fulfilled the inclusion criteria, of which 3 (5.26%) yielded a significant p-value less than α = 0.05. These conclusions were based on n1 = 3439 and n2 = 3508 participants. 22 A reasonable estimate of the datagoverning standardised effect size based on the original findings in Zhong and Liljenquist (2006, Study 2) is within (0.909, 1.429). As before, we err on the side of caution by using the lower bound for our design. A classical power analysis shows that for δ min = 0.909 and power = 80% power, the one-sided p < α test should be conducted after gathering data from n1 = n2 = 16 participants in each group. Lines 2 to 3 of R Code 4.1 with deltaMin <-(4.95-3.75)/1.32 show that with the correspondingly tuned mom E-process, we should plan for n plan = n1 = n2 = 27 participants in each group to observe e n ≥ 20 with 80% power under δ min = 0.909. If δ = δ min the procedure is expected to stop after testing n mean = 17 participants in each group. 23 For most replication attempts the one-sided e-value test hit 22 Unfortunately, we were unable to retrieve the correct "bogota" data from the data sets uploaded at https://osf.io/8cd4r/, which is why our results differ slightly compared to what is reported in Klein et al. (2018). 23 For a two-sided e-value test we require n plan = 33 and the procedure stops after nmean = 21 in each group n plan . We acquired e n ≥ 1/α = 20 in one out of a total of 57 replication attempts (1.75%) based on a total of n1 = 1427 and n2 = 1441 participants per group, thus, n1 = 25.04 and n2 = 25.28 on average. If the magnitude of the data governing δ was indeed at least δ min , then we have provided such δ ample chance to yield persuasive evidence. Since this did not occur for most replication attempts, we infer that the postulate δ ≥ δ min is unlikely with high probability. Moreover, we come to this conclusion with 2012 and 2067 fewer participants per group, which is a reduction of 58.5% and 58.9% from the total sample sizes used in the original analysis. We would like to reiterate the point that with e-values we do not have to stop at the first-passage time e n ≥ 1/α, nor at n plan . Due to E-processes being robust to any stopping time, there is no need to discard newly available data once the test is conducted or the confidence interval is computed, as is the case with a classical analysis.

5 Summary, discussion and concluding remarks

The fact that classical p < α tests and confidence intervals are only reliable when performed once -and only once -places undue pressure on well-intentioned researchers who seek to uphold the highest standards of research practice by pre-registering their confirmatory analyses. Determining the appropriate sample size at which such a test or confidence interval should be computed is difficult. This is especially true before experimentation has started when no data are present.

This problem is circumvented with e-value based methods, which can be used flexibly, allowing us to adapt the experiment to new information as they become available. Only a few lines of code suffice, e.g. R Code 2.1, to prospectively derive a power analysis with a non-binding planned sample size n plan based on an optimal (by default) mom type t-test E-process. Analogous code can be used to construct optimal savi z-tests (i.e. designSaviZ), savi tests for two proportions (Turner, Ly, & Grünwald, 2024) (i.e. designSaviTwoProportions), savi logrank test (Ter Schure et al., 2024) (i.e. designSaviLogrank), and many more are scheduled to be implemented into the safestats R package (Ly, Turner, et al., 2026).

Ville's inequality guarantees that the probability of misleading evidence larger than 1/α remains bounded by α, despite flexible use of the optimised E-process. Simulations under the alternative showed an increase in power compared to classical methods on average. This increase in power translates into smaller expected sample sizes, which further highlights the non-binding nature of n plan . The ability to derive more reliable conclusions with less data allows us to save time, money and effort that can be effectively allocated to other research endeavours. For one set of replication attempts from the Many Labs 2 project, the use of an optimised E-process allows the number of participants to be reduced by more than 70% (Section 4.1), whereas it can result in a reduction of 58% in another set (Section 4.2). These conclusions should not be viewed as criticism on the Many Labs 2 project, which did not exclusively focus on replicating a particular effect efficiently, but also aimed to examine the variation in replicability across samples and settings. Once the data are collected, it is best to use them all, yielding narrower anytime-valid confidence intervals, thus, more precise inference of the effects of interest.

Figure 12

Standard fixed sample estimators, such as the sample mean xn for the population mean µ are biased upwards when they are applied to sample paths that stop as soon as e N ≥ 1/α or at n plan . The dark blue line represents the mean estimate, whereas the broken grey curve represents the true value of µ.

5.1 Summary of (dis-)advantages of e-values

While anytime-valid inference methods provide clear advantages, they also introduce certain costs, most of which are manageable but worth acknowledging. We already discussed some of these points in the preceding text; for clarity, we summarise them here: 1.

Sometimes more data are needed after all In settings where the evidence cannot be monitored and the test is designed for a single application, sequential methods may be less powerful compared to conventional p-value approaches (e.g. Fig. 9). The phenomenon we observed for the t-test holds for many other models as well, such as the logrank test (Ter Schure et al., 2024) or contingency tables (Turner et al., 2024). To obtain a desired power, less data than for a p < α test is needed on average, but more (typically between 40 and 70%) is needed in in the worst-case, namely, n plan.foot_17

2. Biased estimation

If a data-dependent stopping time is used, then standard point estimators of effect sizes may become biased for small sample sizes, see Fig. 12 and the discussion in Section 1.3.6. Unbiasedness is not promised by e-value methods, but they do promise to yield intervals that forever contain the true value with the advertised probability. For large sample sizes, these intervals are guaranteed to yield unbiased estimates, as long as the interval widths shrink over time, as is the case for the mom type E-process. The development of estimators that account for the bias due to using a specific data-dependent stopping time is still a subject of ongoing research.

3. No evidence in favour of the null

The reciprocal of a Bayes factor is known to be interpretable as evidence in favour of the null against the alternative. For instance, BF10 = 1/7 is interpreted as the data being seven times more likely under the null than under the alternative. Despite its interpretability, this does not automatically imply that Ville's inequality holds in the other direction, not even if the Bayes factor is an E-process. 25 In other words, evidence as conveyed with an E-process not only requires interpretability, but also calibration in the frequentist sense (Chugg et al., 2026). Nevertheless, as more and more data become available, the anytime-valid confidence interval provided by our E-process may get narrower and narrower around 0, and if this happens (as it does e.g. in Section 4.2 for the Macbeth effect), we may still informally conclude that 'to all practical intents and purposes, it seems the null is true' -just as is done in the context of classical frequentist confidence intervals.

4. Choice of E-process/prior

When using e-value based inference, choices must be made regarding both the type of E-process to rely on (Section 3), and the tuning parameter within that type. These choices thus introduce a level of subjectivity to the analysis, which might be viewed as undesirable: suppose a research group performs a psychological experiment and reports both the raw data and the corresponding evidence in terms of e-values; then a second group re-analyses the same data. The second group gets a different result, if they use a different tuning parameter. In simple settings, such as with t-tests, there is essentially only one reasonable p-value procedure, so both groups arrive at the same result. While this may seem preferable, we should add that the classical t-test is not without a similarly subjective choice either: instead of choosing an E-process type and tuning parameter, we then have to choose the fixed sample size n at which the analyses will be performed based on a power analysis (van Zwet et al., 2023). Two different research groups doing both the experimental design and analysis will most likely get to quite a different sample sizes, and hence different results. This subjective component becomes invisible when only one group gets to design the experiment, but it is there nevertheless.

It is also important to note that while the tuning parameter in the selection of the E-process takes the form of a prior distribution, the subjectivity it introduces differs fundamentally from that in Bayesian methods. Specifically, the type I error guarantees of e-value based methods holds regardless of the choice of E-variable or prior: altering the prior affects only the statistical power, not the validity of the results. Similarly, the choice of prior affects the width of an anytime-valid confidence interval, but it never compromises its validity. By contrast, in Bayesian statistics, a badly informed prior leads to a bad posterior, and, subsequently, bad credible intervals see Grünwald (2023) and Appendix I and Fig. I² for further details.

Point 4 suggests that the methodology may be vulnerable to cherry-picking biases. To ensure reliable inference with e-values, it is crucial to pre-register the chosen E-process, its tuning parameter, and the confirmatory hypotheses in advance. What is not required, however, is strict adherence to a sampling plan, or even the specified significance thresholds.

We elaborate on this latter advantage below (cf. point ii) in the list of additional benefits of e-values.

i. Choice of E-process/prior

The choice of priors can be viewed as an advantage, as it enables the integration of existing data to inform the design of E-processes. Once chosen, the ensuing savi tests and anytime-valid confidence intervals will remain statistically sound, and the better the prior was informed, the narrower the confidence interval will tend to be (Grünwald, 2023).

ii. The e-value as a continuous measure of evidence against the null; roving α Like Bayes factors, e-values can be interpreted as a measure of evidence against the null hypothesis. They have a clean 'evidential' interpretation, independent of the chosen α, for example as the money won in a betting game where no money is expected to be gained if the null hypothesis is correct. This evidential interpretation is worked out in detail by Chugg et al. (2026), who also show as Wagenmakers (2007) and many others did, that the evidential interpretation of p-values is quite problematic. Whereas the practice of setting α post-hoc equal to the observed p-value is a mortal sin, setting α post-hoc equal to the reciprocal of the observed e-value is meaningful (Grünwald, 2024; Koning, 2024).

iii. E-value based multiple testing/nonparametrics Waudby-Smith and Ramdas (2024) showed that E-variables require far fewer samples to reach the same statistical power as existing Bayesian or classical methods in certain nonparametric settings, where distributional assumptions are minimal or absent (in such settings, they take on a form that looks quite different from a likelihood ratio). Their impact is equally transformative in multiple testing scenarios, where e-values are driving what can be described as a paradigm shift in the field (R. Wang & Ramdas, 2022; Xu et al., 2025).

iv. Meta-Analysis Point 1 above shows how e-value-based methods might require more samples in the planning stage within a single study. In contrast, the case for evalue methods is strongest in reproducibility studies such as Many Labs 2, where experiments are repeated many times. Then the Law of Large Numbers kicks in and the average, rather than the worst-case, will determine what happens: Across studies, the e-value-based method almost certainly requires fewer data points than classical tests. This principle also applies to meta-analyses, where e-values prove especially valuable (Grünwald, De Heide, & Koolen, 2024; Ter Schure et al., 2022). In such settings, a meta-analytical e-value can be defined to robustly aggregate evidence as data accumulate across multiple studies. The approach ensures type I error control, even when individual studies trigger replication attempts, differ in sample size, and are on-going.

The list above shows that e-values are far more than a tool for anytime-valid testing -they represent a new paradigm of inference, not just "yet another method". Their key property is flexibility: they adapt not only to different sampling plans, but also enable the incorporation of prior knowledge and to data-dependent adjustments of significance thresholds. This flexibility simplifies statistical practice. By contrast, classical approaches impose rigid constraints that often obstruct, rather than advance scientific progress. Reproducible and generalisable science certainly requires effort, but that effort should not be squandered on upholding outdated, rigid sampling plans that e-values render unnecessary.

5.2 Future Directions

In the present work we focussed on only null hypothesis testing problems. Hence, data collection is stopped once the evidence against the null hypothesis, quantified by the e-value for significance when it crosses 1/α. However, researchers are often also interested in testing whether the effect size is actually relevantly large. If it is not, then we can also stop the experiment early and declare the effect uninteresting. We already alluded to this possibility in Section 3.5, where we described an approach to do this based on anytimevalid confidence intervals. However, the underlying E-processes are usually not optimised for this task. A more suitable approach is to construct a specialised E-process against the hypothesis that the effect is relevantly large that is optimised to quickly gain evidence for the null, whenever the null holds true. Data collection can then be stopped as soon as either the "standard" process exceeds 1/α or when the relevance process exceeds 1/α R . The choice of suitable relevance processes is the subject of a forthcoming paper.

A further subject for future research is the development of savi tests for other standard testing problems in the social sciences. There are currently savi versions available for the logrank test (Ter Schure et al., 2024), Fisher's exact test (Turner et al., 2024), Student's t-test, and linear regression (Lindon et al., 2026). Savi tests for AN(C)OVA problems and generalised linear models such as logistic regression under a special 'Model-X' condition (Grünwald, Henzi, & Lardy, 2024) are currently under development. Other standard testing problems for which there are, to our knowledge, no savi tests available include (hierarchical) general and generalised linear models without 'model-X', and non-parametric tests such as the Mann-Whitney U -test or the Kruskal-Wallis test.

For the counter example below, we choose a1 = 3.98, g1 = 0.03, a2 = 4.02, g2 = 0.05, a0 = 4, g0 = 2 and α σ = 2 and β σ = 1/2. An interpretation for this choice is as follows: If there is a difference in the population mean then it is relatively small with population means at 3.98 and 4.02 and this knowledge is quite concentrated. On the other hand, if the null holds true, then the shared mean is 4 and the prior is relatively spread out. Depending on the values of these 8 tuning parameters, perhaps due to how they were estimated using past data, the procedure that stops as soon as the BF Left panel: The FPR of monitoring the BF10 ; η ≥ 1/α test (dashed brown) does not remain below α = 0.05. It contradicts Ville's inequality and therefore cannot be an E-process. Right panel: The coverage rate of the (Bayesian) credible interval (dashed brown) dips below the nominal 95%-level, if it is used sequentially, and actually mimics the behaviour of a frequentist confidence interval, see Fig. I² below.

Ville's inequality must hold. This reflects a point made earlier by De Heide and Grünwald (2021) that there can be issues with Bayes factors under optional stopping. We introduced the example in this section to show that type I error control due to Ville's inequality does not automatically hold for all Bayes factors in general. The constructed Bayes factor in Fig. I² in this case is not an E-process because it violates Property (iii) (Eq. ( 5)) by having conditional expectations larger than one for at least one data generating distribution from the null model. The right panel of Fig. I1 shows that the coverage rate of the associated 95% credible interval will also drop well below the nominal level of 95%. Fig. I² shows the typical evolution of the chosen computationally convenient Bayes factor BF10 for the same data (under the null) that were used for Fig. 2. The right panel of Fig. I² shows three types of intervals in one plot. The (Bayesian) credible interval (yellow) quickly intersects with the classical confidence interval (red), and both unfortunately do not cover the true mean difference, here, φ = 0 at all times, whereas the anytime-valid confidence interval (blue) does. Hence, as with 95% confidence intervals, we cannot guarantee that the 95% (Bayesian) credible interval covers the true underlying parameter with 95% probability during data collection or at a possibly data-driven stopped time. This is unfortunately a fundamental problem; Some Bayes factors are E-variables, but no credible interval forms a sequence of anytime-valid confidence intervals. The problem cannot be solved by choosing different priors, as typical priors yield credible intervals that (relatively quickly) converge to, and thus behave as, classical confidence intervals (Ghosal & van der Vaart, 2017).

An anytime-valid confidence interval based on e-values avoids being turned into a classical confidence interval by not updating a prior to a posterior as is the case for credible intervals, but by inverting the savi test. This is, thus, a completely different procedure yielding wider intervals as shown in Fig. I². We feel that the additional width is a relatively Left panel: The (non-default) computationally convenient Bayes factor (Appendix H) tends to overstate the evidence against the null. Right panel: The (Bayesian) credible interval (yellow) and the classical confidence interval (red) both do not cover the true data generating mean difference of zero at all times, unlike the anytime-valid confidence interval (blue), which is also a wee bit wider.

low price to pay for reliability and convenience, as it is guaranteed that the true underlying parameter value is covered with 95% probability regardless of when or even if data collection has stopped. The resulting anytime-valid confidence interval in some cases still has some special, though, non-standard Bayesian interpretation (Pawel, Ly, & Wagenmakers, 2024).

Appendix J Anytime-valid tests are more flexible compared to sequential Bayes factor tests In the t-test setting certain Bayes factors, such as the one discussed in Section 1.3.5, are also E-variables. The way they are used in forming a sequential Bayes factor test, however, differs from that of a savi test based on Protocol 1.1. Sequential Bayes factor tests (e.g. Schönbrodt, Wagenmakers, Zehetleitner, & Perugini, 2017 , Schnuerch et al., 2022 , Pramanik & Johnson, 2022) follow Protocol 7.1. This procedure does not come with an n plan at which the detection of an effect |δ| ≥ δ min with at least, e.g. power = 80% is guaranteed. Crucially, it also has an additional evidence boundary for accepting the null. The latter is natural due to a Bayes factor less than 1 being interpreted as evidence for the null.

The current discussion in sequential Bayes factor testing revolves around (1) the selection of the boundaries A and B, thus, a specific stopping rule, and (2) which Bayes factor to use. Type I and II errors are estimated based on simulations that depends on specific choice, most importantly, the chosen stopping rule. For E-processes the stopping time is irrelevant, which makes its use easier and more straightforward.

To address the boundary question, Schönbrodt et al. (2017) suggested employing the eCauchy E-process/Bayes factor t-test, constructed with a Cauchy prior on δ s , featuring a prior width of 1, and decision boundaries set to A = 6 and B = 1/6 for early lines of research. This general recommendation was based on a large-scale simulation study with data-governing δ = 0.5, for which this particular Bayes factor and boundaries resulted in realised type I and II error rates of 4.7% of 4.6%, respectively.

Inspired by Wald's sequential probability ratio test, Schnuerch et al. (2022) argue for the boundaries A = 1-β α and B = β 1-α instead. These boundaries were also explored by Pramanik and Johnson (2022) who recommend using the non-local moment prior, that is, the mom E-process with the two bumps at ±0.3 as default. Their large-scale simulation studies show that the resulting sequential Bayes factor test yields average sample sizes close to those of the two-point prior variant at ±0.3. In the large-scale simulation studies the realised type I and type II error were observed to be below the tolerable α and β respectively. This can partially be explained by the employed Bayes factors also being E-processes, at least as far as the type I error is concerned.

The Wald boundaries on their own, however, do not actually guarantee error control. For instance, running Protocol 7.1 with boundaries A = 1-β α = 16, B = β 1-α = 0.21 and the computationally convenient Bayes factor of Eq. (H4) leads to 16.8% correct null acceptance, but 24.9% false positive rejections, and no conclusion was reached for the remaining 58.2%. The latter inconclusive category can lead to this procedure requiring more data points than the savi testing procedure.

Stopping rule dependent error control make the procedure less flexible. For instance, during data acquisition a hopeful investigator changes his mind and continues sampling despite observing BF10 ≤ 0.21 by interpreting BF10 = 0.21 as only "moderate evidence" for the null (e.g. Jeffreys, 1961 , Appendix B, Lee & Wagenmakers, 2013). Continuing sampling after hitting the lower boundary makes the sequential Bayes factor Protocol 7.1 equivalent to the savi testing Protocol 1.1 that has the potential to run indefinitely due to the absence of an n plan . By Ville's inequality we know that halting as soon as BF10 ≥ 16 only guarantees a type I error of 6.25% under the null, if the Bayes factor is also an E-process. If it is not, we cannot even say this, as is the case for the computationally convenient Bayes factor. Assuming the Bayes factor is an E-process, a type I error guarantee of level α = 0.05 therefore forces the investigator to stop as soon as BF10 ≤ 0.21 so that the unaccounted probability of 1.25% can be absorbed by the lower boundary. E-processes are robust to adaptation of the stopping rule, as it still defines a stopping time for which Ville's inequality holds. This makes e-value based inference more flexible.

The additional stopping rule for the null also complicates the design of sequential Bayes factor tests. To the best of our knowledge, all sequential Bayes factor tests require extensive simulations to provide an indication of both the realised type I and type II error, and they will all have to be re-performed when the stopping rule or the tuning parameter of the Bayes factor under consideration is changed. In contrast, type I error control for Eprocesses is mathematically guaranteed by Ville's inequality, and type II error control and estimates of the run times of experiments can be obtained with much cheaper simulations. Specifically, e-value sampling designs provide an indication of how long the experiment should run for in terms of n plan , and monitoring the test until n plan guarantees a 1 -β probability to detect effects |δ| ≥ δ min . This n plan , in turn, is found by relatively quick simulations for typical values of α, β and δ min.

Appendix B Markov's inequality

Let X be a non-negative random variable having distribution P, then for any c > 0 Markov's inequality states that

The principle is that if the non-negative random variable X has a low expectation, then large values cannot occur often. If extreme values do occur often, then its expectation cannot be that low. For instance, assume that the population average is E P [X] = 1, then the event X ≥ 90 cannot occur with probability 0.8. To see this, we reason to a contradiction. Note that the smallest value X for which P(X ≥ 90) = 0.8 holds occurs when all 80% concentrates at X = 90. This minimal case already contributes 72 to the average of X and we are left with a probability of 0.2. Since X is non-negative the smallest value is 0 and giving that 20% yields 0. Summing the two shows that P(X ≥ 90) = 0.8 implies that the average of X is at least 72, which contradicts the E P [X] = 1 assumption. More formally, we recall that the expectation/population average is a weighted average, i.e. E P [X] = ∫ xp(x)dx, where the integral goes from zero to any c > 0 and then to ∞, thus,
