Appendix D Likelihood ratios are E-processes

We verify the claim that for any density q( is an E-process of the simple null P .

Non-negativity follows from the fact that both p and q are non-negative. At n = 1, we have hence, E q starts at one.

To verify Eq. ( 5), we write N for an arbitrary stopping time. The proof hinges on the fact that the null model is simple, the law of total probability, and that a realised stopping time of N = m corresponds to outcomes x m belonging to some (measurable) set B m . As an example, we consider the z-test with variance known to be one, thus, z ∶= √ nx n , where xn is the sample mean of n data points. As an example stopping time we take the first time N at which p < 0.05, which corresponds to observing |x n | > 1.96 √ n . The event N = m is equivalent to data sequences x m = (x 1 , . . . , x m ) with sample means |x j | < 1.96 √ j for j = 1, . . . , m -1, otherwise m would not be the first time to stop, and |x m | > 1.96 √ m , otherwise we would not have stopped at time m. The collection of all these data sequences x m equals B m . Regardless of the stopping time N , there exists a corresponding B m.

Before applying the law of total probability, we note that where 1 {N = m} = 1 if the stopping time realises m, and zero otherwise.

In other words, E q N equals E q m whenever N = m as one would expect. The law of total probability allows us to compute the expectation E P 0 [E q N ] in two steps: The integral over the outcomes of X m conditioned on the event {N = m} and a (green) expectation E P 0 pertaining to the outcomes of N , that is,

For Eq. ( D4) we took 1 {N = m} out of the inner integral, because it is a function of the conditioning event {N = m}. For Eq. ( D5) we use the fact that we can swap sums and expectations of non-negative functions.

As before, the expectation E P 0 [E q m | {N = m}] defines an integral. However, by conditioning on N = m the possible outcomes of length m becomes restricted to an event B m . Continuing from Eq. ( D5), and exploiting the fact that E q m is a ratio of densities that can be factorised via conditioning, we have that where again a function of the conditioning event can be taken out of the expectation.

Using the fact that B m corresponds exactly to the outcomes of X m given B m we can conclude that the inner integral equals one, that is,

The last equality follows from the fact that the probability of any N taking on any outcome is one, in particular, under the measure Q corresponding to the numerator of E q n . Because N was taken arbitrarily and we only have to deal with a single P 0 , we verified that Eq. (??) indeed holds for simple likelihood ratios.

For those interested in the technicalities, the defining property Eq. ( 5) of E-processes holds generally for any non-negative supermartingales that starts at 1, and it is known as Doob's optional stopping theorem. In other words, non-negative supermartingales are Eprocesses. However, not every E-processes is a non-negative supermartingale, see Ruf et al. (2023) for the details.

Appendix E Safe anytime-valid t-tests

The challenge of composite null models. The concept of likelihood ratio becomes ambiguous when M 0 consists of multiple distributions, say, parametrised by σ with densities f (x n | σ), e.g. the normal distribution centred at zero with σ > 0 unknown. For concreteness, we assume that the alternative extends the null with an additional parameter, say, δ, thus, f (x n | σ, δ).

From a practical point of view of wanting a single number for a given data set x n , the frequentist plugs in the maximum likelihood estimator based on x n for the unknown parameters, whereas the Bayesian integrates them out. This leads to the generalised likelihood ratio statistic , and the Bayes factor where δ1 , σ1 are the unrestricted, and σ0 the restricted maximum likelihood estimates, and π 1 and π 0 are priors chosen by the statistician.

Neither of these two are in general Eprocesses. Reduction to a simple null model by transforming the data. One trick involves transforming the data such that the composite null becomes simple. We elaborate on this computationally intensive technique in the one-sample t-test case with X i iid ∼ N (σδ, σ 2 ), where δ = µ/σ and δ = 0 under the null. To reduce the null model, we consider the transformed data points W i ∶= X i /|X 1 |, which makes (i) the first data point trivially 1 or -1 with probability 1/2 under the null, and (ii) the other data points dependent on the magnitude of the first observation. A careful computation (Hendriksen, 2017) then shows that, under the null, the sampling distribution of W n with the first trivial data point marginalised out is given by where νs 2 w ∶= ∑ n i=1 (w i -w) 2 and w ∶= 1 n ∑ n i=1 w i .

Note that the sampling distribution of w is independent of σ. Hence, the transformation makes the null model simple. As such, likelihood ratios with p(w n ) in the denominator and any density q(w n ) of the transformed data w n is an E-process. Because the ultimate expressions depend only on the T -statistic, we do not see the dependence on w n , as t = √ n w/s w = √ nx/s.

Appendix G Why Reporting the Running Maximum Amounts to Cheating

In this appendix we explore some subtleties concerning data dredging. For concreteness, consider the following scenario: suppose a researcher initially followed Protocol 1.1, with α = 0.05 and n plan = 100. His E-process never achieved 1/α = 20 before or at n plan . Upon reaching sample size n plan , he stopped and noticed that the E-process achieved its maximum at, say, N = 37, when it obtained value e37 = 17. After that, the e-values kept fluctuating; in particular, they reached a minimum of, say e95 = 2 and then increased again until e100 = 10.

The researcher may now be tempted to remove the consecutive block of outcomes X 38 , X 39 , . . . , X 94 from his sample. Re-calculating the e-values on the modified data sequence X 1 , X 2 , . . . , X 37 , X 96 , . . . , X 100 , he finds that the final e-value is now 85, thus much larger than 1/α, so he can claim a rejection after all. This is a serious form of data-dredging: the resulting output is not a valid e-value any more, and the probability, under the null, that, after removing 'the least favourable block of subsequent outcomes' from the data, the resulting 'e-value' is larger than 1/α, may very well be much larger than α -the type I error guarantee is violated.

Next, consider a milder form of data dredging: another researcher, confronted with the same data as above, might simply claim, a bit more modestly, that she stopped at N = 37, obtaining an e-value of e37 = 17. This procedure of reporting the running maximum results in an invalid e-value, and thus amounts to cheating, for the simple reason that she determined N retroactively, after having observed more data. Thus, she claims an output e N at a time N that is not a bona fide stopping time so it does not give us a valid E-variable.

While this reasoning is correct, there is a subtlety, since one might counter: doesn't Ville's inequality tell us that the probability, under the null, that E n will ever cross 1/α bounded by α? So what harm can there be in reporting its maximum value observed so far? After all, the type I error guarantee still holds! While the observation that E n will, with high probability under the null, never cross 1/α is indeed correct (and in this sense the violation is less severe than the previous one), something can still go wrong. To see why, consider the following follow-up scenario: after our researcher reported e37 = 17, the result was reported in a technical report and she turned her attention to other projects. Yet, one year later, a new team of researchers, at a different lab, considers the same hypothesis test; they note that 'the threshold was almost achieved' and re-start data collection in as similar circumstances as possible, and keep updating their e-value, starting with the original researcher's 37 data points with e-value e37 = 17. Then, effectively, they generate data points X 101 , X 102 , . . . and so on, and when, say, they cross the 1/α threshold after 15 new examples, what they have done really amounts to having thrown away all data points in-between X 37 and X 100 -and thus, they have ended up in the more serious form of data-dredging after all, which does break the type I error guarantee. In other words, reporting the maximum e-value observed so far does preserve type I error guarantee if you have an absolute guarantee that the experiment forever be stopped, but it gets messed up under optional continuation. More generally, as soon as the 'evidence' output by a researcher cannot be interpreted any more as the value of an E-process at a legitimate stopping time, there is a risk that, if data collection continuous, the type I error guarantee gets violated at some point in time.

Appendix H A computationally convenient (two-sided) two-sample t-test

A Bayes factor is by definition a ratio of marginal likelihoods, where the marginalisation is with respect to a pair of priors, one for each model (e.g. Jeffreys, 1961 , Ly et al., 2016a , 2016b).

In the two-sample t-test setting the numerator has a likelihood function that depends on, say, µ 1 , µ 2 , σ, whereas the denominator has a likelihood function that only depends on two parameters, say, the global mean µ g and σ. The priors π 1 (µ 1 , µ 2 , σ) and π 0 (µ g , σ) used to construct Bayes factor in favour of the alternative over the null for data x (n) is then

A computational convenient choice is to exploit conjugacy, which states that normal likelihoods N (x (n) | µ, σ) for data x (n) combined with normal priors lead to normal posteriors.

Appendix I Not all Bayes factors are E-processes

Frequentist principles such as power and type I error control are the main motivation for this work. It is therefore surprising that ensuring these frequentist principles over time led us to E-processes that were previously derived as Bayes factors, as remarked in Section 1.3.5. The distinction in their definitions is striking: An E-process is characterised by constraints on the expectation under every data-generating P and all possible stopping time (see Eq. ( 5)), whereas a Bayes factor is defined as a ratio of marginal likelihoods, which integrates out the parameters in the numerator and the denominator (e.g. Eq. ( H1)). Grünwald, De Heide, and Koolen (2024) provides insights to when the two concepts align. Roughly speaking, optimal E-variables have a Bayes factor representation, because they are derived from projecting a (mixture) alternative model onto the so-called convex hull of the null model. By convex we mean a weighted average of P ∈ M 0 , thus, a mixture, or a marginal likelihood. In this view, the prior is used as means to solve a minimisation problem and does not represent prior belief.

It is, however, not true that every E-process is a Bayes factor, nor is every Bayes factor an E-process. Firstly, E-processes derived with the universal inference construction are based on plugins, which cannot be written as a ratio of marginal likelihoods (e.g. Pandeva, Bakker, Naesseth, & Forré, 2024 , and Ramdas et al., 2023 , but also Turner et al., 2024). Secondly, the computationally convenient Bayes factor BF10 ; η , i.e. Eq. (H4), is an example

We hope that the code provides some additional insights to this phenomenon for those who are unfamiliar with it. All R code used for the simulations and plots can be found in the OSF repository https://osf.io/mdbqe/files/osfstorage.

The value of the curve at n stems from tallying the number of p-value sequences that, up to time n, led to a false rejection of the null hypothesis. The number of false positives is then divided by the total number of p-value sequences resulting in the relative frequency of interest. Similarly, the red curve in the right panel of Fig.3represents the fraction of replicating data sets that included the true mean difference of µ1 -µ2 = 0 at all times up to n = n1 = n2.

For example, the existence of just one black swan is enough to disprove the statement "all swans are white".

There exists E-processes that are robust to real outliers(Saha & Ramdas, 2025), but researchers must decide in advance (ideally through pre-registration) whether to use such a procedure, as recommended below.

To ease exposition, we write maximum, but it should in fact be a supremum (the smallest upper bound). Similarly, below we write a minimum, which should actually be an infimum (the largest lower bound). The differences between maximum/minimum and supremum/infimum matter substantially on a technical level, seeGrünwald, De Heide, and Koolen (2024) andLarsson et al. (2025) for further details.

These optimal solutions are derived by Taylor expanding the logarithm of the E-variables for ν large, integrating with respect to t, followed by differentiating with respect to the tuning parameter, i.e. gmom, g, κ, see Appendix E.

For those interested to code along, we recommend installing version 0.8.8 or higher of the safestats package in R.

Hence, in terms of confidence interval widths there is no procedure that outperforms another at all time points simultaneously.

The lower (upper) bound equals the observed mean difference 5.29-3.86 divided by the largest (smallest) sample standard deviation. Typical estimates divide the mean difference by some type of average between the two standard deviations.
